## Other public services (`otherServices`)

*Setting, unit % of GDP, default 0, range -3 to 3 in steps of 0.1.*

Real change in other public services: administration, police, culture, roads.

**Definition.** Level shift in real spending on other public services, % of baseline GDP a year, persistent while set, split between staff and purchases as at baseline. Setting it back to 0 returns spending to baseline. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and output and unemployment keep drifting too, for decades: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6), and the króna follows domestic prices. After 20 years on Manual, income tax +2.5 points leaves output about 3.8% lower (0.6% after a year, 2.1% after five) and unemployment 1.2 points higher, both still moving, with the price level 16% lower and the króna 23% stronger; VAT +2.5 points leaves output 2.1% lower and the price level 7% lower, so on Manual a VAT rise ends up lowering prices; health spending −3% of GDP leaves output 6% lower and unemployment 3.4 points higher; public investment −3% leaves output 9% lower. Treat Manual effects beyond a few years as a picture of an economy without its nominal anchor. On Automatic the central bank’s rule, which slowly learns its neutral rate, brings inflation back: after 20 years within about 0.05 point for VAT and the income-tax offset ±2.5, about 0.05 for transfers, but benefits +30 points leave unemployment about 1 point higher, and public spending ±3% of GDP about ±0.2 (investment) to ±0.8 point (education) of inflation and unemployment 0.3–1.3 points off normal, because public services move jobs more than output and the rule’s estimate of the neutral rate reaches its limit.

Runs: -3 % of GDP (min); -0.8 % of GDP (down); 0.8 % of GDP (up); 3 % of GDP (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -3 % of GDP (min): Household consumption (real) -0.21 on Manual, 0.23 on Automatic; Exports (real) -0.32 on Manual, 0.14 on Automatic; Bank capital ratio -0.03 on Manual, 0.10 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -0.8 % of GDP (down): Household consumption (real) -0.06 on Manual, 0.06 on Automatic; Exports (real) -0.08 on Manual, 0.04 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 0.8 % of GDP (up): Household consumption (real) 0.06 on Manual, -0.06 on Automatic; Exports (real) 0.08 on Manual, -0.04 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 3 % of GDP (max): Household consumption (real) 0.22 on Manual, -0.23 on Automatic; Exports (real) 0.31 on Manual, -0.15 on Automatic; Bank capital ratio 0.03 on Manual, -0.11 on Automatic.

Expectations:

- ✓ output rises over months 1–24 (max, any): 3, Manual: 2.62; 3, Automatic: 1.98. Government purchases add directly to demand and output. (Blanchard and Perotti (2002); Ramey (2019))
- ✓ output falls over months 1–24 (min, any): -3, Manual: -2.63; -3, Automatic: -1.99. Cutting government purchases lowers demand and output. (Guajardo, Leigh and Pescatori (2014))
- ✓ unemployment falls over months 1–24 (up, any): 0.8, Manual: -0.44; 0.8, Automatic: -0.40. Higher public spending raises employment (Okun’s law). (Okun (1962))
- ✓ govBalance falls over months 1–24 (max, any): 3, Manual: -2.44; 3, Automatic: -2.88. Higher spending worsens the budget balance. (Government budget identity)
- ✓ imports rises over months 1–24 (max, any): 3, Manual: 1.87; 3, Automatic: 1.77. Import leakage from purchases and from the extra income. (Batini et al. (2014))
- ✓ keyRate rises over months 3–36 (max, Automatic): 3, Automatic: 2.22. The Taylor rule leans against a demand expansion. (Taylor (1993))

## Public investment (`publicInvestment`)

*Setting, unit % of GDP, default 0, range -3 to 3 in steps of 0.1.*

Real change in public investment: roads, buildings and equipment, bought from builders, who import part of it.

**Definition.** Level shift in real public investment, % of baseline GDP a year, persistent while set; nominal spending moves with domestic prices. The government pays builders, who import part of what they install at the import share of all investment. Public capital is not modelled, so the road or school adds nothing to what the economy can produce, and on Manual a lasting rise crowds out private spending through prices: +0.8% of GDP leaves real household consumption about 0.2% lower after three years and still lower after twenty. Setting it back to 0 returns it to baseline. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and output and unemployment keep drifting too, for decades: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6), and the króna follows domestic prices. After 20 years on Manual, income tax +2.5 points leaves output about 3.8% lower (0.6% after a year, 2.1% after five) and unemployment 1.2 points higher, both still moving, with the price level 16% lower and the króna 23% stronger; VAT +2.5 points leaves output 2.1% lower and the price level 7% lower, so on Manual a VAT rise ends up lowering prices; health spending −3% of GDP leaves output 6% lower and unemployment 3.4 points higher; public investment −3% leaves output 9% lower. Treat Manual effects beyond a few years as a picture of an economy without its nominal anchor. On Automatic the central bank’s rule, which slowly learns its neutral rate, brings inflation back: after 20 years within about 0.05 point for VAT and the income-tax offset ±2.5, about 0.05 for transfers, but benefits +30 points leave unemployment about 1 point higher, and public spending ±3% of GDP about ±0.2 (investment) to ±0.8 point (education) of inflation and unemployment 0.3–1.3 points off normal, because public services move jobs more than output and the rule’s estimate of the neutral rate reaches its limit.

Runs: -3 % of GDP (min); -0.8 % of GDP (down); 0.8 % of GDP (up); 3 % of GDP (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Asymmetry** (Automatic): Broad money (bank deposits) 0.02 for 0.8 vs -0.09 for -0.8 (4.0×).
- **Mode sign**: -3 % of GDP (min): Real house prices -0.96 on Manual, 0.11 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) -1.88 on Manual, 1.66 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -0.8 % of GDP (down): Real house prices -0.25 on Manual, 0.03 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) -0.49 on Manual, 0.45 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 0.8 % of GDP (up): Real house prices 0.25 on Manual, -0.03 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) 0.48 on Manual, -0.46 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 3 % of GDP (max): Real house prices 0.94 on Manual, -0.14 on Automatic; Bank capital ratio 0.08 on Manual, -0.02 on Automatic; Profits, exporters (real) 1.78 on Manual, -1.77 on Automatic.

Expectations:

- ✓ output rises over months 1–24 (max, any): 3, Manual: 2.10; 3, Automatic: 1.61. Public investment raises demand; its domestic content raises output at once. (IMF WEO Oct 2014, ch. 3 (Abiad et al. 2016))
- ✓ output falls over months 1–24 (min, any): -3, Manual: -2.10; -3, Automatic: -1.63. Cutting public investment lowers demand and output. (IMF WEO Oct 2014, ch. 3)
- ✓ investment rises over months 1–60 (max, any): 3, Manual: 16.7; 3, Automatic: 14.2. Public investment is part of gross fixed capital formation. (SNA 2008 national-accounts identity)
- ✓ imports rises over months 1–12 (max, any): 3, Manual: 3.28; 3, Automatic: 3.30. Investment goods have high import content in a small open economy. (OECD TiVA (import content of investment))
- ✓ currentAccount falls over months 1–12 (max, any): 3, Manual: -1.56; 3, Automatic: -1.49. Sectoral balances: a larger public deficit with extra imports worsens the current account (twin deficits). (Godley and Lavoie (2007); Abbas et al. (2011, IMF Econ Review))
- ✓ govBalance falls over months 1–24 (max, any): 3, Manual: -2.81; 3, Automatic: -3.09. Higher spending worsens the budget balance. (Government budget identity)
- ✓ keyRate rises over months 3–36 (max, Automatic): 3, Automatic: 1.72. The Taylor rule leans against a demand expansion. (Taylor (1993))
- ✓ unemployment falls over months 3–36 (up, any): 0.8, Manual: -0.21; 0.8, Automatic: -0.15. Construction output raises employment with a lag (Okun’s law). (Okun (1962); IMF WEO Oct 2014, ch. 3)

## Old-age and disability transfers (`oldAgeTransfers`)

*Setting, unit % of GDP, default 0, range -2 to 2 in steps of 0.1.*

Real change in public pensions and disability benefits (mostly to older people).

**Definition.** Level shift in real old-age and disability transfers, % of baseline GDP a year, indexed to the CPI and split by age as at baseline; persistent while set. Setting it back to 0 ends it. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and output and unemployment keep drifting too, for decades: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6), and the króna follows domestic prices. After 20 years on Manual, income tax +2.5 points leaves output about 3.8% lower (0.6% after a year, 2.1% after five) and unemployment 1.2 points higher, both still moving, with the price level 16% lower and the króna 23% stronger; VAT +2.5 points leaves output 2.1% lower and the price level 7% lower, so on Manual a VAT rise ends up lowering prices; health spending −3% of GDP leaves output 6% lower and unemployment 3.4 points higher; public investment −3% leaves output 9% lower. Treat Manual effects beyond a few years as a picture of an economy without its nominal anchor. On Automatic the central bank’s rule, which slowly learns its neutral rate, brings inflation back: after 20 years within about 0.05 point for VAT and the income-tax offset ±2.5, about 0.05 for transfers, but benefits +30 points leave unemployment about 1 point higher, and public spending ±3% of GDP about ±0.2 (investment) to ±0.8 point (education) of inflation and unemployment 0.3–1.3 points off normal, because public services move jobs more than output and the rule’s estimate of the neutral rate reaches its limit.

Runs: -2 % of GDP (min); -0.5 % of GDP (down); 0.5 % of GDP (up); 2 % of GDP (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -2 % of GDP (min): Króna value 0.15 on Manual, -0.08 on Automatic; Exports (real) -0.03 on Manual, 0.03 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -0.5 % of GDP (down): Króna value 0.04 on Manual, -0.02 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 0.5 % of GDP (up): Króna value -0.04 on Manual, 0.02 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 2 % of GDP (max): Króna value -0.15 on Manual, 0.08 on Automatic; Exports (real) 0.03 on Manual, -0.03 on Automatic.

Expectations:

- ✓ consumption rises over months 1–24 (max, any): 2, Manual: 1.10; 2, Automatic: 0.99. Transfers raise recipients’ disposable income, and they spend part of it (MPC). (Parker, Souleles, Johnson and McClelland (2013, AER); Wilcox (1989, JPE))
- ✓ consumption falls over months 1–24 (min, any): -2, Manual: -1.10; -2, Automatic: -0.99. Cutting transfers lowers recipients’ income and spending. (Parker et al. (2013))
- ✓ realDisposableIncome rises over months 1–12 (max, any): 2, Manual: 2.44; 2, Automatic: 2.40. A transfer is household income: disposable income rises by the after-tax amount. (SNA 2008 household income account)
- ✓ output rises over months 3–24 (max, any): 2, Manual: 0.50; 2, Automatic: 0.37. The transfer multiplier: extra spending by recipients raises output, by less than purchases do. (Coenen et al. (2012, AEJ Macro))
- ✓ govBalance falls over months 1–24 (max, any): 2, Manual: -1.14; 2, Automatic: -1.18. Higher transfers worsen the budget balance. (Government budget identity)
- ✓ govDebt rises over months 12–60 (max, any): 2, Manual: 2.47; 2, Automatic: 3.07. Persistent deficits accumulate into debt. (Government budget constraint)
- ✓ keyRate rises over months 6–36 (max, Automatic): 2, Automatic: 0.57. The Taylor rule leans against the rise in demand. (Taylor (1993))

## Family and housing benefits (`familyBenefits`)

*Setting, unit % of GDP, default 0, range -2 to 2 in steps of 0.1.*

Real change in child, parental-leave and housing benefits (young and working age).

**Definition.** Level shift in real family and housing benefits, % of baseline GDP a year, indexed to the CPI and split by age as at baseline; persistent while set. Setting it back to 0 ends it. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and output and unemployment keep drifting too, for decades: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6), and the króna follows domestic prices. After 20 years on Manual, income tax +2.5 points leaves output about 3.8% lower (0.6% after a year, 2.1% after five) and unemployment 1.2 points higher, both still moving, with the price level 16% lower and the króna 23% stronger; VAT +2.5 points leaves output 2.1% lower and the price level 7% lower, so on Manual a VAT rise ends up lowering prices; health spending −3% of GDP leaves output 6% lower and unemployment 3.4 points higher; public investment −3% leaves output 9% lower. Treat Manual effects beyond a few years as a picture of an economy without its nominal anchor. On Automatic the central bank’s rule, which slowly learns its neutral rate, brings inflation back: after 20 years within about 0.05 point for VAT and the income-tax offset ±2.5, about 0.05 for transfers, but benefits +30 points leave unemployment about 1 point higher, and public spending ±3% of GDP about ±0.2 (investment) to ±0.8 point (education) of inflation and unemployment 0.3–1.3 points off normal, because public services move jobs more than output and the rule’s estimate of the neutral rate reaches its limit.

Runs: -2 % of GDP (min); -0.5 % of GDP (down); 0.5 % of GDP (up); 2 % of GDP (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -2 % of GDP (min): Króna value 0.22 on Manual, -0.12 on Automatic; Exports (real) -0.04 on Manual, 0.04 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -0.5 % of GDP (down): Króna value 0.06 on Manual, -0.03 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 0.5 % of GDP (up): Króna value -0.06 on Manual, 0.03 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 2 % of GDP (max): Króna value -0.22 on Manual, 0.12 on Automatic; Exports (real) 0.04 on Manual, -0.04 on Automatic.

Expectations:

- ✓ consumption rises over months 1–24 (max, any): 2, Manual: 1.65; 2, Automatic: 1.49. Benefits to young families (high MPC, often liquidity-constrained) raise their spending. (Parker et al. (2013); Kaplan and Violante (2014, Econometrica))
- ✓ consumption falls over months 1–24 (min, any): -2, Manual: -1.65; -2, Automatic: -1.50. Cutting benefits lowers recipients’ income and spending. (Parker et al. (2013))
- ✓ realDisposableIncome rises over months 1–12 (max, any): 2, Manual: 3.43; 2, Automatic: 3.37. A transfer is household income. (SNA 2008 household income account)
- ✓ output rises over months 3–24 (max, any): 2, Manual: 0.75; 2, Automatic: 0.56. Transfer multiplier through recipients’ spending. (Coenen et al. (2012))
- ✓ govBalance falls over months 1–24 (max, any): 2, Manual: -1.58; 2, Automatic: -1.64. Higher transfers worsen the budget balance. (Government budget identity)
- ✓ govDebt rises over months 12–60 (max, any): 2, Manual: 3.30; 2, Automatic: 4.26. Persistent deficits accumulate into debt. (Government budget constraint)
- ✓ keyRate rises over months 6–36 (max, Automatic): 2, Automatic: 0.85. The Taylor rule leans against the rise in demand. (Taylor (1993))

## Unemployment-benefit rate (`unemploymentBenefits`)

*Setting, unit pp of wage, default 0, range -30 to 30 in steps of 5.*

Changes the replacement rate paid automatically to the unemployed.

**Definition.** Level shift in the replacement rate, in percentage points of the average wage, applied to everyone unemployed at once and persistent while set. It works two ways. At once, the unemployed have more to spend, which supports demand and jobs (an automatic stabiliser). Within about a year, people out of work search longer before taking a job and workers hold out for more pay, so normal unemployment rises: +30 points raises unemployment by about 0.6 points over years 5–20 on Manual and 0.9 on Automatic. Who ends up working depends on the mode. On Automatic the central bank leans against the higher normal unemployment, so fewer people work: jobs about 0.2% and output about 0.4% lower after twenty years. On Manual, with the key rate held, the benefits’ support to demand wins: jobs end about 0.3% and output 0.85% higher, while more people search longer (a known gap: search and bargaining theory predict fewer jobs). Setting it back to 0 ends it the same way. With the key rate held (Manual), a lasting change that keeps unemployment off its normal rate keeps inflation off target, and output and unemployment keep drifting too, for decades: expectations are only half anchored to the target, so the long-run Phillips curve is not vertical (decision 0002 §6), and the króna follows domestic prices. After 20 years on Manual, income tax +2.5 points leaves output about 3.8% lower (0.6% after a year, 2.1% after five) and unemployment 1.2 points higher, both still moving, with the price level 16% lower and the króna 23% stronger; VAT +2.5 points leaves output 2.1% lower and the price level 7% lower, so on Manual a VAT rise ends up lowering prices; health spending −3% of GDP leaves output 6% lower and unemployment 3.4 points higher; public investment −3% leaves output 9% lower. Treat Manual effects beyond a few years as a picture of an economy without its nominal anchor. On Automatic the central bank’s rule, which slowly learns its neutral rate, brings inflation back: after 20 years within about 0.05 point for VAT and the income-tax offset ±2.5, about 0.05 for transfers, but benefits +30 points leave unemployment about 1 point higher, and public spending ±3% of GDP about ±0.2 (investment) to ±0.8 point (education) of inflation and unemployment 0.3–1.3 points off normal, because public services move jobs more than output and the rule’s estimate of the neutral rate reaches its limit.

Runs: -30 pp of wage (min); -10 pp of wage (down); 10 pp of wage (up); 30 pp of wage (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Expectations:

- ✓ unemployment rises over months 60–240 (max, any): 30, Manual: 0.56; 30, Automatic: 0.88. A higher replacement rate lengthens job search and raises reservation wages, so equilibrium unemployment rises. (Layard, Nickell and Jackman (1991), Unemployment; Mortensen and Pissarides (1994))
- ✓ unemployment rises over months 60–240 (up, any): 10, Manual: 0.20; 10, Automatic: 0.29. A higher replacement rate lengthens job search and raises reservation wages, so equilibrium unemployment rises. (Layard, Nickell and Jackman (1991), Unemployment; Mortensen and Pissarides (1994))
- ✓ unemployment falls over months 60–240 (min, any): -30, Manual: -0.68; -30, Automatic: -0.87. Less generous benefits shorten job search, so equilibrium unemployment falls. (Layard, Nickell and Jackman (1991), Unemployment; Mortensen and Pissarides (1994))
- ✓ employment falls over months 60–240 (max, Automatic): 30, Automatic: -0.13. A higher reservation wage raises the rate of unemployment at which inflation is steady, and an inflation-targeting central bank does not hold unemployment below it, so fewer people work. (On Manual, with the key rate held, the benefits’ support to demand outweighs this: lever-vetting open item on benefits.) (Layard, Nickell and Jackman (1991), Unemployment; Mortensen and Pissarides (1994), Review of Economic Studies)
- ✓ output falls over months 120–240 (max, Automatic): 30, Automatic: -0.26. Fewer people in work produce less once policy has leaned against the higher normal unemployment. (Layard, Nickell and Jackman (1991), Unemployment)

## Who buys new government bonds (`bondBuyers`)

*Choice, unit choice, default 0.*

Banks and the central bank pay with newly created money; pension funds and households pay with existing deposits.

**Definition.** Choice, persistent while set: every new bond sold from then on goes to the chosen buyer, or 40/60 to banks and pension funds in the mix. Pension funds and older households buy only what their deposits can pay for that month; banks take the rest. When the budget is in surplus the government buys bonds back from every holder in proportion to what they hold, whatever the choice. Bonds already sold stay where they are, though pension funds and older households slowly sell surplus bonds to banks to restore their portfolio shares. New bonds pay the key rate plus its spread whoever buys, so the choice changes money and who receives the interest, not interest rates. The central bank earns the bond rate on the bonds it buys and hands its profit to the government, but it pays the key rate on the reserves it creates to buy them, so on those bonds the government saves only the bond rate’s spread over the key rate (0.5 points), not the whole interest bill: on Manual, after 20 years of 2% of GDP more public investment, the budget balance is about 0.1% of GDP better and debt about 0.7 points of GDP lower than with the mix. Non-residents are not an option: they buy and sell bonds with banks on their own, through the carry trade.

Runs: Banks; Central bank; Pension funds; Older households. Each is set before month 1 and held.

Also run on top of a companion shock, **Public investment 2 % of GDP** (`publicInvestment`), because the choice acts only on new bonds, and the baseline budget balances, so none are sold without a deficit. Those runs are measured against the run with the companion shock alone, in the same mode, and follow the plain runs.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Inert**: none of its 8 runs moves a headline or an indicator by 0.005 or more in any month.

Expectations:

- ✓ broadMoney rises over months 1–60 (1, any, with the companion shock): 1, Manual: 2.02; 1, Automatic: 2.49. When banks buy the new bonds, the deposits the government spends are not drained, so broad money rises compared with the 40/60 mix. (McLeay, Radia and Thomas (2014, BoE Quarterly Bulletin Q1); Godley and Lavoie (2007))
- ✓ broadMoney rises over months 1–60 (2, any, with the companion shock): 2, Manual: 1.99; 2, Automatic: 2.52. Central-bank purchases create reserves, and the spent deposits stay in the economy, as under QE. (McLeay, Radia and Thomas (2014))
- ✓ broadMoney falls over months 1–60 (3, any, with the companion shock): 3, Manual: -1.35; 3, Automatic: -1.66. Non-bank buyers pay with existing deposits, which drains the money the deficit adds. Broad money is lower than under the mix, where banks take 40%. (McLeay, Radia and Thomas (2014); Godley and Lavoie (2007))
- ✓ broadMoney falls over months 1–60 (4, any, with the companion shock): 4, Manual: -1.31; 4, Automatic: -1.64. Households pay with existing deposits: money moves rather than being created. (McLeay, Radia and Thomas (2014))
- ✓ govDebt falls over months 24–240 (2, any, with the companion shock): 2, Manual: -0.37; 2, Automatic: -0.19. Interest on central-bank-held bonds returns to the Treasury as central-bank profit (consolidation), so deficits and debt are lower. (Buiter (2007, ‘Seigniorage’, Economics e-journal))
- ✓ output does not move over months 1–24 (1, any, with the companion shock): 1, Manual: 0; 1, Automatic: 0. When the central bank sets the interest rate, who holds the bonds changes money but not the cost of credit, so demand is essentially unchanged in the short run. (McLeay, Radia and Thomas (2014); Borio and Disyatat (2010, BIS WP 292))

## Pension funds’ foreign allocation (`pfForeign`)

*Setting, unit pp of assets, default 0, range -20 to 20 in steps of 1.*

Shifts the target foreign share of pension assets; funds move toward it through new flows, limited by their cash, selling krónur, and the weaker króna revalues what they hold. They also want fewer domestic assets: they lend less of new mortgages, and covered bonds and mortgages cost a little more.

**Definition.** Level shift in the target foreign share, in percentage points of assets, persistent while set. Funds close the gap at up to 0.5 a year, but they buy only with cash above their buffer: new contributions, income, and the bank bonds, deposits and mortgage lending they give up. Shifts of a few points move at about that pace (+5: about 3 points higher after a year and 4 after two). Larger ones are cash-limited for years: at +20 the share is about 7.5 points higher after a year, about 10 after two and 13.5–15 after five (less on Automatic, where the higher key rate slows the króna’s fall). Part of the rise is the weaker króna raising the value of the foreign assets they already hold. While set, the funds want proportionally less of every domestic asset: they lend a smaller share of new mortgages (banks lend the rest), and covered bonds and mortgages carry a funding premium of about 0.15 pp per 5 points. In the first two years the weaker króna lifts inflation, and with it banks’ capital (their indexed loans grow with the CPI), so banks give a small discount on loans that offsets part of the premium: indexed mortgage rates rise about half as much as covered-bond rates at first. Setting it back to 0 makes them sell foreign assets back toward the old share, and the premium goes.

Runs: -20 pp of assets (min); -5 pp of assets (down); 5 pp of assets (up); 20 pp of assets (max). Each is set before month 1 and held.

Comparisons between runs:

- **Mode sign**: -20 pp of assets (min): Investment (real) -0.20 on Manual, 0.10 on Automatic; Current account 0.04 on Manual, -0.24 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: -5 pp of assets (down): Investment (real) -0.05 on Manual, 0.02 on Automatic.
- **Mode sign**: 20 pp of assets (max): Investment (real) 0.15 on Manual, -0.07 on Automatic.

Expectations:

- ✓ krona falls over months 3–36 (up, any): 5, Manual: -5.45; 5, Automatic: -4.74. Portfolio balance: pension funds sell krónur to buy foreign assets, so the króna weakens. (Kouri (1976); Branson (1977); CBI and pension funds’ March 2020 pause of FX purchases to support the króna)
- ✓ krona rises over months 3–36 (down, any): -5, Manual: 6.85; -5, Automatic: 5.84. Repatriation means buying krónur, so the króna strengthens. (Kouri (1976); Branson (1977))
- ✓ inflation rises over months 3–24 (up, any): 5, Manual: 0.61; 5, Automatic: 0.53. Exchange-rate pass-through: a weaker króna raises import and consumer prices. (Central Bank of Iceland QMM documentation; Campa and Goldberg (2005), Review of Economics and Statistics)
- ✓ currentAccount rises over months 3–60 (up, any): 5, Manual: 0.64; 5, Automatic: 0.75. Balance-of-payments identity: a private financial outflow is matched by a current-account surplus, through expenditure switching. (Godley and Lavoie (2007), Monetary Economics, ch. 12)
- ✓ exports rises over months 3–36 (up, any): 5, Manual: 2.36; 5, Automatic: 2.07. A weaker real króna makes exports more competitive. (Marshall-Lerner and expenditure switching; Dornbusch (1976))
- ✓ imports falls over months 3–36 (up, any): 5, Manual: -1.92; 5, Automatic: -2.03. A weaker real króna makes imports dearer, so volumes fall. (Expenditure switching; Dornbusch (1976))
- ✓ pfForeignShare rises over months 6–240 (up, any): 5, Manual: 4.63; 5, Automatic: 4.50. Raising the target foreign share raises the actual foreign share as the funds rebalance. (Stock-flow portfolio adjustment (Tobin 1969))
- ✓ keyRate rises over months 6–36 (up, Automatic): 5, Automatic: 1.10. An inflation-targeting central bank raises its rate against depreciation-driven inflation. (Taylor (1993); Central Bank of Iceland inflation-targeting framework)
